Local Land Use Regulation
•
•
•

•

The City of Monticello began an evaluation of land use regulation for data center
development in 2024, following a series of inquiries related to data center development.
Throughout 2025 and into 2026, the City held a series of public meetings to discuss
development of data center uses in Monticello.
The meetings resulted in amendments to the Monticello 2040 Vision + Plan to allow data
center land uses in light industrial areas and the adoption of zoning ordinances to
regulate data center development.
To inform these decisions, the City completed significant research on data center
development. Sources included government, academic, industry, site visits, and news
media, reviewed in the interest of addressing the potential problems, solutions, and
benefits of data center development.

3

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

•
•

•

•

•

•

•
•

•

The City used this research, paired with feedback from the community, to construct its
land use policies and regulations for data center development.
The City ﬁrst addressed data center land use policy within the 2040 Vision + Plan
(Comprehensive Plan). City boards and staﬀ use the Comprehensive Plan to guide land
use decisions. The City adopted an amendment to the Plan allowing data center land
uses in its Light Industrial Park land use designation, with companion criteria for data
center land uses.
In the 2040 Plan policy discussion, the City recognized that data center development
generally creates fewer permanent jobs compared to other industrial development of
similar size. Jobs tend to be technical and can be variable given the frequent need to
update technology. Construction of larger data center facilities often generates
signiﬁcant regional and local employment, spread over multiple years.
The 2040 Plan policy discussions considered the ﬁscal beneﬁts of large-scale data center
development. Data centers have the potential to generate property tax benefits with a
comparably lower impact on police, fire, and similar public services. Data center
development also adds to a more stable and diverse tax base for Monticello.
After adoption of the 2040 Plan amendment for data center land uses, the City drafted
zoning ordinances speciﬁc to data center development. Zoning ordinances regulate land
development in support of the City’s land use goals and policies.
In April of 2026, the City adopted a zoning district for data center development. The
zoning district is known as the Data Center Planned Unit Development District (DCPUD).
The ordinance requires:
o Any data center development to pay for all costs attributable to its development.
o Any data center development to mitigate foreseeable impacts on neighboring
property, including noise, light, environmental concerns, public utility
infrastructure, and the implications of a long-term construction timeline.
o Any data center proposal to be evaluated for impact on the City’s supply of
industrial land and its ability to meet other land use goals. These include
maintaining resource capacity, ensuring land supply for the community’s other
goals, and advancing Monticello as a more resilient community.
The DCPUD ordinance applies to all data center development within the city.
Use of a Planned Unit Development (PUD) district allows the City to address variation in
the types and scale of data center development and the potential impacts of hyperscale
data center projects. A PUD also recognizes that data center development often includes
a variety of supporting activities and structures within a defined area.
Development of an individual data center or data center campus requires approval of
rezoning to DCPUD. An ordinance speciﬁc to the proposal is adopted as part of the
4

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

•

•

rezoning, including both the DCPUD ordinance’s general standards and standards
speciﬁc to the proposal.
Data center development within the approved DCPUD requires a Conditional Use Permit
(CUP). The Conditional Use Permit process requires additional site development
information. With the CUP, the City also sets conditions for how a data center site will
develop.
Both the DCPUD rezoning and DCPUD Conditional Use Permit processes require a public
hearing at the Planning Commission before decision by the City Council.

Current Applications
•
•

•

•
•

•

•

The City received an application for a large-scale data center campus, commonly
referred to as a “hyperscale data center,” in July of 2026.
The application encompasses an area of approximately 547 acres. The land area is within
the Monticello Orderly Annexation Area (MOAA), but outside current city boundaries.
The property owners in the area have petitioned for annexation to the City. The City
may process land use applications once the subject property is petitioned for
annexation to the City.
The proposed data center applications request a zoning amendment (rezoning)
establishing a Data Center Planned Unit Development District (DCPUD), rezoning of
other smaller parcels in the application area to Industrial Business Campus (IBC) or
Agriculture-Open Space (A-O), a Conditional Use Permit for data center campus
development, a preliminary plat to subdivide the proposed development property, and a
ﬁnal plat organizing the area into lots, blocks and outlots.
The City rejected the applications submitted in July as incomplete, citing application
deﬁciencies and requiring additional information.
The applicant revised and supplemented the application materials, resubmitting their
application requests. The City found the resubmitted rezoning, CUP and preliminary plat
applications complete for review on August 11, 2026. The ﬁnal plat application was
rejected as premature and is not part of the current application review.
Once land use applications are complete, the City is required by state statute (Chapter
§15.99) to make a decision on the applications within a set amount of time. The City’s
deadline for action on these requests is December 8, 2026. The preliminary plat is
subject to §462.358 for decision timeline. The City’s deadline for action on the
preliminary plat request is December 8, 2026.
The Planning Commission holds the required hearings on the current applications and
makes a recommendation to the City Council. The City Council decides whether to
approve or deny the current applications, associated contracts, and the annexation
petitions.
5

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

•

•

The City has engaged with several agencies, including the City’s consulting civil and
environmental engineer, a sound engineer, Xcel Energy, the Wright County Highway
Engineer, the Minnesota Department of Natural Resources (DNR), and the Minnesota
Department of Transportation (MnDOT) as part of its review process.
The City completed an AUAR (Alternative Urban Areawide Review) for the data center
area. An AUAR is an environmental review process for identifying and mitigating
potential environmental impacts of development. The format and process for an AUAR
are deﬁned in Minnesota Rule Chapter 4410.3610. The Monticello Industrial AUAR
evaluates two development scenarios for the subject area, including a scenario for
development of up to 3 million square feet of data center/technology campus. The
AUAR provides the City with a Mitigation Plan for development of this area. The land
use applications received and development on site are reviewed for compliance with
the Mitigation Plan.

Overview of Application Narrative and Plans
•

Rezoning
o The application proposes three separate zoning districts. Of the approximately
547 acre area, the primary development area is approximately 370 acres and is
proposed to be rezoned as DCPUD. This zoning district would contain the data
center buildings, support facilities, and related infrastructure, including an
electrical power substation.
o The application proposes two outlots totaling 131.5 acres along the southeast
and south boundaries of the site, straddling existing water basins. The applicant
has proposed a zoning designation of Agriculture-Open Space (A-O) for this area.
These parcels will also be included within a Shoreland Overlay District upon
annexation per DNR requirements. Stormwater, trail, and utilities are proposed in
these outlots.
o The third zoning area proposed is in the northwest corner of the site at the
intersections of Trunk Highway 25, 85th Street NE and Davidson Avenue NE. This
approximately 11-acre area is proposed to be zoned Industrial and Business
Campus (IBC).
o The preliminary plat subdivision includes two outlots and a lot to be conveyed to
the City for a future water tower site in the IBC area. The applicant’s maximum
water consumption threshold will not require a new well or new water tower.
The City’s Comprehensive Water System Plan, which provides long-range capital
improvement planning for the City’s water system, identifies the need for a
future water tower location in the south-central portion of the City’s growth
area.
6

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

•

•

•

•

Access
o There are three proposed access points to the site. This includes the primary site
access point from 85th Street NE, a secondary access point along the extension of
Davidson Avenue NE, and a third access point from Edmonson Avenue NE
speciﬁc to the proposed substation. (Edmonson Avenue is also known as County
Rd. 117 where it is subject to Wright County jurisdiction.)
o The City is recommending the extension of Davidson Avenue NE to 85th Street as
a frontage road with the subdivision process. The extension provides a local
alternative to TH25 for local traﬃc moving through the area. Davidson Avenue is
proposed to be platted as public right of way and includes the extension of
municipal utilities to serve future development of parcels located adjacent to
Davidson Avenue and to the south along TH 25.
Screening & Landscaping
o The DCPUD ordinance requires a screening berm and its plantings to be installed
in their entirety as a ﬁrst phase of site work, prior to initiation of other private
improvements on the site. The application shows a berm of 10 feet in height
surrounding the “developable area” of the 370-acre parcel. The berm height will
require adjustment in some areas to meet the ordinance standard for 10 feet
above adjoining road centerline or property line.
o The berm is shown to be planted with trees and shrubs that extend the screening
eﬀect beyond height of the berm.
o Speciﬁc standards for management of the proposed tree canopy and species
throughout the site are outlined in detail in the report and conditions of approval
provided in this report.
Construction Management
o Construction staging and activities must be located within the bermed/screened
area with the proposed DCPUD-zoned area or receive separate permitting from
the appropriate governmental jurisdiction or agency.
o Speciﬁc standards for construction management are outlined in detail in the
report and proposed ordinance.
Public Utilities & Infrastructure
o The project will utilize a closed-loop cooling process system using no more than
75,000 gallons per day of domestic water based on an annual average. This
amount is below the State of Minnesota’s water appropriation permit threshold
for a data center site and the requirement for amendment to the City’s current
permit. The exceptions to the average daily use limit of 75,000 gallons per day
are limited to charging of the closed-loop system and temporary irrigation for
7

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

establishment of vegetation. The site will utilize municipal water service and no
new well service is required.
o Wastewater discharge is limited to on-site sanitation and domestic discharge at
no more than 75,000 gallons per day based on an annual average. The discharge
of wastewater threshold is not correlated to the closed-loop system for water
demand and instead relates to expected domestic wastewater discharge to the
municipal system.
o The applicant proposes to pay for the required public improvements necessary to
extend services to the site, without which the annexation and development of
the property will be premature. These improvements are shown in preliminary
detail in the application materials. The improvements will be installed by the City
and include:
 Trunk sanitary sewer extension from Chelsea Road along Fallon Avenue
NE and 85th Street NE to the site.
 Trunk watermain extension from Chelsea Road along Fallon Avenue and
85th Street NE to the site.
 Construction of a new roundabout at School Boulevard and Fallon
Avenue.
 Reconstruction of Fallon Avenue NE to an urban section and abutting
trails from Chelsea Road to 85th Street NE.
 Full reconstruction of 85th Street NE to an urban section from Fallon
Avenue NE to Edmonson Avenue NE, except where already improved. A
section of 85th Street NE is planned to be relocated with the planned
construction of a roundabout at 85th Street NE and Fallon Avenue. The
timing of this improvement is unknown and may be coordinated with the
completion of the 85th Street NE improvements in this area.
 Intersection control improvements at 85th Street NE and TH25 pending
the outcome of a MnDOT-required Intersection Control Evaluation.
• The applicant also proposes a series of developer-installed public improvements
adjacent to the site, which are shown in the application materials:
 Full reconstruction of 85th Street NE to urban section with abutting
trail from Edmonson Avenue NE to TH 25, along with water and sewer
extensions, except where already improved.
 Water and sewer extensions and construction of pathway from 85th
Street NE along Edmonson Avenue to the southerly water basin area
per Wright County rural section standards.

8

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

Construction of Davidson Avenue NE to an urban section from current
terminus to 85th Street NE, including extension of public utilities to
the south edge of the site for future growth.
 Water and sewer utility line extension across the south edge of the
site in a dedicated right-of-way, including construction of a trail for
utility service access. This trail will also serve as an important
greenway corridor connection.
 Internal site circulation and any required watermain looping is subject
to a required Site Plan review.
The project proposes to use a diesel backup generator system totaling less than 25 MW,
below the State of Minnesota’s threshold for additional environmental review. The
DCPUD ordinance also sets speciﬁc requirements for backup generators that are
applicable to all data center development, including the proposed.
The applicant has submitted an initial ambient noise study. Staﬀ recommend conducting
an additional ambient noise study given the outcomes of the prior study as related to
traﬃc noise and duration of measurement. The additional study will provide more
accurate benchmark measurements for future sound studies required by the DCPUD
ordinance. This additional ambient noise study would be required prior to the sound
study required prior to construction of the required berm.
The proposal states that the data center campus will require power capacity in excess of
500 MW (megawatts). The applicant has completed a System Impact Study to verify
power service sufficient to meet this capacity, per application narrative. Xcel Energy has
confirmed completion of the study per the applicant narrative. A Facility Study is in
progress by Xcel Energy to determine necessary infrastructure to support service to the
site. Transmission lines are stated as required for service to the site. Routing for these
transmission line corridors is regulated by the Public Utilities Commission (PUC). The City
of Monticello will have the opportunity to comment on the routing as part of the public
permit process through the PUC. Xcel Energy has indicated that the cost for the studies
and power infrastructure service for the facility are the responsibility of the
developer/user of the site.
The staﬀ report which follows provides detailed technical analysis of the above and
other ordinance requirements and site considerations.

PROJECT DESCRIPTION
The submitted applications propose development of a Data Center Campus, with a series of
buildings devoted to Data Center uses. Data Center and Data Center Campus are deﬁned by the
Monticello Zoning Ordinance [See Exhibit R]. The applications also request the platting and
zoning of the proposed smaller lots and outlots for the subject property.
The subject property is approximately 547 acres in total. It is located generally south of 85th
Street NE, east of Trunk Highway 25, west of Edmonson Avenue NE, and extending south to a
public water basin.
The proposed data center project includes a combination of data center buildings between
approximately 2,000,000 and 3,000,000 square feet of total ﬂoor area. The application narrative
indicates the potential for approximately 5-10 principal buildings ranging from 200,000-400,000
square feet in size. The site would also include several accessory uses and supporting facilities
and structures, including an electrical substation.
The data center facility is proposed to be constructed in a single continuous construction
phased over 4-8 years.
REGULATORY ENVIRONMENT
Minnesota Land Use Statutes
The City manages land use pursuant to authority granted by the State of Minnesota in Minn.
Statutes Chapter 462, and other relevant sections.
Under Minnesota law (Chapter §15.99), the City has up to 120 days (60 days, plus an option for
a city-initiated 60-day extension) to consider and act on any complete application for zoning.
The City also has a maximum of 120 days to consider and act on any complete application for
preliminary plat (Chapter §462.358).
The City’s land use review process is only one component of the regulations which may be
applicable to a given data center development, as outlined below.
Minnesota Statutes – Chapter 12 - House File 16
In 2025, the State of Minnesota adopted legislation which provides specific requirements for
data center development, including large-scale data center facilities. This legislation provides a
significant review and regulatory framework for any large-scale data center development. The
regulations include water appropriations, energy rate structure and review, and annual fee
payments to the State of Minnesota to be directed to the Energy & Conservation Account.

11

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

State Agency Roles
Many concerns commonly cited for data center development are regulated in Minnesota by a
state agency. Data center development may require review by multiple state agencies as
described below. The submitted applications are reviewed in relationship to state agency
requirements in this report, or in companion comments by the City Engineer’s oﬃce.
Public Utilities Commission: Electrical Power Supply
The Minnesota Public Utilities Commission (PUC) is the primary agency responsible for
regulation of electrical power for data center development. The PUC monitors expansion
of the electrical transmission system with power providers. The Commission ensures
that the electrical grid is not compromised by large power users and further regulates
the provider’s provision of power to a site. The PUC manages the process by which
major improvements and powerline extensions are considered and permitted. In
addition, any generation of power a capacity greater than 25 MW (megawatts) on a
project site is subject to PUC review and permitting. The 25MW capacity threshold is
also an MPCA review threshold for air emission review.
The 2025 state legislation also requires any electrical generation, distribution, or other
costs attributable to electric service for data center development to be borne by the
data center project and not spread to ratepayers. This rate or “tariﬀ” structure is also
reviewed by the PUC. [See Exhibit Q]
Department of Natural Resource: Water Supply
The Minnesota DNR regulates the extraction of water from aquifers, monitoring when
large users may aﬀect groundwater and aquifer supplies. For the proposed data center,
the applicant has indicated that they will consume no more than 75,000 GPD (gallons
per day) based on an annual average, which is under DNR permit thresholds for
appropriation permit or any changes to the City’s current water appropriation.
Moreover, this total is less than the current agricultural irrigation well permit
appropriation. Existing irrigation wells will be required to be capped.
The DNR may also review development applications when subject to speciﬁc shoreland
or wetland regulations, as applicable.
Pollution Control Agency: Noise
The Minnesota Pollution Control Agency has adopted regulations related to the
generation of noise. Those standards are identiﬁed in Minnesota Rules Chapter 7030.
Cities may not adopt regulations that alter these noise levels and limitations. However,
the City can adopt more limited time periods for certain types of activity, such as
construction. Monticello’s DCPUD ordinance includes limitations on construction activity,
12

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

in addition to requirements to verify compliance with MPCA standards. These include
studies to establish baseline conditions, before construction of operations generating
site noise, and during operations throughout the life of the project.
MPCA is also involved in stormwater management and air quality for speciﬁc projects
and thresholds.
Department of Transportation: Traﬃc
MnDOT regulates access to the state Trunk Highway and receives required notice of any
plat proposed abutting its highway. As part of plat review, MnDOT may require
additional study to manage traﬃc and access.
Local Land Use Regulation
Comprehensive Plan
In Monticello, land use is guided by an adopted Comprehensive Plan. The current
Comprehensive Plan is the “Monticello 2040 Vision + Plan.” The Comprehensive Plan
lays out the goals, policies, and strategies that the City expects to follow for the term of
the “planning horizon,” typically about 20 years. The goals and policies are implemented
through the City’s land use ordinances, primarily the zoning and subdivision chapters.
In its long-range planning, Monticello has long focused on a strong business and
industrial sector, balanced by a full range of housing opportunities. The 2040 Vision +
Plan continues that strategy. Commercial and industrial growth has been key to
providing business services to its residents, attractive employment opportunities, and
tax base growth and diversification. The Comprehensive Plan also directs land use policy
toward sustainable and incremental growth. Incremental and sustainable growth
requires that the City carefully consider annexation so as not to “leapfrog” to unserved
lands, ensure that annexation and development of land is consistent with land use
guidance, and require that development considered for the City’s growth area pays for
itself. This policy is reflected in the City’s annexation agreement with Monticello
Township and Wright County.
Balancing contiguity, utility access, and land use compatibility for potentially disruptive
land uses is a priority for siting industrial land use . The City has chosen to guide its
largest areas of industrial land near the limits of the MOAA, in the northwest areas of
the MOAA and the area currently under consideration in an eﬀort to balance these
considerations.
Over the past few years, the City has received interest in data center development in the
community. Given the potential scale and scope of these types of projects, Monticello
concluded that as adopted, the Comprehensive Plan did not adequately address the
13

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

potential impacts of data center development on the community, or on the economic
development goals of the City. The City Council decided to explore the potential beneﬁts
and impacts on the community and how the Comprehensive Plan should address the
use.
Staﬀ and city oﬃcials researched data center uses and their impacts in an intensive way:
(1) examining the state of the industry as it has developed in other areas, (2) reviewing
academic, governmental, industry, and news articles on data center projects, (3) visiting
several data center sites in the upper Midwest, (4) interviewing staﬀ members and
oﬃcials in cities with existing or developing data center projects, and (5) consulting with
industry experts and engineers.
The research process uncovered examples of poorly implemented data center projects –
those consuming power that caused shortages or raised electricity rates, those that
consumed prodigious amounts of water stressing aquifer supplies and wastewater
treatment, those that generated noise and other physical impacts to nearby
neighborhoods. Some of these also created “temporary” negative impacts associated
with construction that lasted for several years. If left unregulated, these attributes
would be incompatible with Monticello's Comprehensive Plan goals of sustainability and
community character.
However, the research also showed there are options to mitigate negative impacts along
with multiple benefits that can be captured. Tax base diversification and contribution is
primary among these, but other community beneﬁts can be leveraged due to the sheer
scale of development, including infrastructure investment and construction employment
that last several years, far beyond the normal short-term impacts of construction. Those
long-term construction jobs translate to a multiplier eﬀect on the local business
economy.
Weighing these beneﬁts and impacts, the City adopted amendments to the 2040 Plan to
accommodate data center development, but only under limited terms related to the
mitigation of negative impacts and achievement of community goals. [See Exhibit M]
Zoning Ordinance
Land use decisions in Monticello are implemented through the zoning and subdivision
Ordinances. These ordinances reﬂect the policy direction of the Comprehensive Plan.
With the adoption of the 2040 Vision + Plan (Comprehensive Plan) amendments, the
City undertook an extensive process of updating its zoning regulations to implement the
Comprehensive Plan objectives for data center development. Rather than incorporate
data center uses into its existing Industrial development zoning, the City initiated a
14

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

process to create a separate zoning district. This district was developed to specifically
address the unique aspects of data center development, addressing identified negative
impacts while providing enough flexibility to accommodate advances in technology over
time, and requiring application information sufficient to evaluate a proposal.
The strategy employed in the resulting zoning district (the “Data Center Planned Unit
Development” District – DCPUD) is to gather an extensive set of baseline data about any
proposal and site, evaluate that data against the City goals and other regulatory agency
requirements, and then provide the City with adequate discretion to build a zoning
district that mitigates the potential negative impacts of the use – whether neighborhood
or community-wide - while capturing the benefits that data center projects can
generate.
The development of the DCPUD zoning district included several public meetings with
extensive opportunity for public participation. The public process generated comments
which ultimately strengthened the ordinance.
The structure of the DCPUD ordinance zoning district has been described as creating a
distinct developable area or “box” within the larger property. Within the developable
area, the developer and/or end user will be able to design and arrange its various
principal buildings, appurtenant uses, and support facilities based on an identiﬁed set of
Minimum Performance Standards. Outside of the developable area “box,” the developer
will design the site to ensure that impacts of the facility – visual, sound, lights, etc. are
virtually indistinguishable from any other development project, and again subject to a
set of Minimum Performance Standards. The PUD process allows the City to manage the
location, elements and impacts of any activity that may be outside of the developable
area, including parking lots, accessory buildings (such as offices or security facilities),
and similar elements. These elements might exist outside of the site’s primary
development area, but still inside the required setback.
The ordinance requires that the site will be designed to ensure that impacts of the
facility – visual, sound, lights, etc. are virtually indistinguishable from any other
development light industrial project. The entire DCPUD site is required to be enclosed by
a heavily planted 10-foot tall berm that fully screens and buﬀers ground-level activities
inside.
The DCPUD ordinance also requires signiﬁcantly greater setbacks for data center
development as compared to other industrial districts. The setbacks require additional
increases when adjoining any residential land use and when supporting activities such as
mechanical equipment, generators or cooling equipment are not further screened by
buildings from adjoining properties.
15

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

Procedurally, the DCPUD requires rezoning to DCPUD and a Conditional Use Permit for
data center development. The DCPUD provides the speciﬁc application requirements
and review process for both.
Under the DCPUD ordinance, a separate zoning district unique to each project is
established as a part of the DCPUD zoning process. This district establishes the speciﬁc
site development area for the DCPUD, as well as any unique aspects of the project given
its setting in the community. It overlays other requirements of the zoning ordinance that
all remain applicable, except where the adopted DCPUD district states otherwise.
The applicant is also required to apply for a Conditional Use Permit (CUP). If approved
the CUP grants the use, outlines any plan corrections or clariﬁcations, and establishes
the conditions for ongoing construction and operation.
The applicant may also need to plat the property, and seek annexation to the City,
depending on proposed location.
The rezoning, CUP and preliminary plat applications proceed through the Planning
Commission at a public hearing, then to City Council for decision. If approved, a
subsequent Final Plat is required, with a development agreement outlining additional
development terms, conditions and ﬁnancial securities.
These application approvals include conditions that must be met prior to proceeding
with the project. No approval is valid and eligible for permitting until all of the conditions
applied to the Council’s decision have been fulﬁlled. Some conditions are ongoing or
operational in nature which requires adequate assurances and securities that the
applicant will fulﬁll them at a future date or continue to meet them throughout the life
of the project.
Site Plan Review
If adopted, the DCPUD ordinance requires submission for Site Plan review. A Site Plan
review is an administrative review process. City departments will review required
submittals against the City’s ordinances, the adopted DCPUD ordinance for the site and
the approved CUP plans. The Site Plan approval process is applicable for each phase of
site development to ensure that each meets all of the applicable conditions and
requirements of the approved DCPUD. This process is required prior to grading,
construction or building permit in any phase. As required by the DCPUD ordinance, the
ﬁrst phase of development in a DCPUD requires construction of the berm and perimeter
screening prior to any other site development work.

16

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

SUBJECT APPLICATIONS AND REQUIRED CITY APPROVALS
Annexation
Approvals for the proposed project require annexation of the site to the City of Monticello,
which is a decision consideration of the City Council. Annexation is governed by the Monticello
Orderly Annexation Area (MOAA) agreement between the City and Monticello Township. The
current property owners have petitioned for annexation, which the City Council has
acknowledged. Under the MOAA agreement, petitions must be for land located within the
designated MOAA boundary, adjacent to the existing City limits, and able to be served by City
utilities.
Annexation consideration by the City Council also requires an annexation agreement that
governs the required public improvements to serve the site, including timeline for the
developer’s submission of securities for the estimated $70 million in oﬀ- and on-site
improvements required to support the proposed development. The securities will be required
to facilitate the City’s process for acquisition of right of way, bid process and installation, and
inspection of improvements. Other agreements related to water and sewer usage may also be
required, with timing based on an understanding of service and operational demands.
The City Council will consider the annexation of the subject site at the time it reviews the zoning
and subdivision applications and will process the annexation in speciﬁc steps as outlined in an
annexation agreement.
Subdivision
The applicant has submitted a preliminary plat application for Edmonson Business Center to
subdivide the subject property into multiple parcels and plat required right of way.
Lot Conﬁguration
•

•

The proposed subdivision includes a primary DCPUD site, an area of approximately
363.6 acres to be platted as Lot 1, Block 1. The location of an electric power
substation is proposed for the parcel shown as Lot 2, Block 1. The applicant indicates
the substation may require adjustment due to the requirements of the electric utility
provider, Xcel Energy or the Public Utilities Commission permit process. The
proposed plat currently provides a 6.6-acre site which may shift in size or precise
location prior to ﬁnal plat.
The subdivision proposes a 2.6-acre parcel in the northwest corner of the site to be
platted as Lot 1, Block 2. The parcel is located at 85th Street NE and Trunk Highway
25. The lot will be dedicated to the City with the subdivision as a future water tower
location.

17

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

•
•

•

The proposed subdivision includes four outlots. By ordinance, outlots are not eligible
for development until they are ﬁnal platted into lots and blocks.
Outlot A is approximately 4.4 acres. It is in the northwest corner of the plat. This
outlot includes buﬀer area created by the extended alignment of Davidson Avenue
NE. Outlot B is in the same general area and is 4.2 acres in size. These parcels are
subject to a future plat and application of ordinances for any development
proposals.
Outlots C and D incorporate the south and southeast portions of the plat that are
subject to the Shoreland Overlay regulations upon annexation. The outlots
encompass an area that extends 1,000 feet from the Ordinary High Water mark of a
designated public water. The DNR public waters basin designation is 394-W. Upon
annexation, the DNR will redesignate the basin to lake status, requiring the
application of the Shoreland Overlay regulations once within the city. The northerly
Outlot C is approximately 101.7 acres, and the southerly Outlot D is approximately
29.8 acres. The two outlots are separated by a public right of way of 150 feet in
width that is a utility and trail corridor.

Right of Way, Utilities and Land Dedication
•

•

•

•
•

The extension of sanitary sewer and watermain along Fallon Avenue and 85th Street
NE is required to serve the site with municipal utilities. Without the extension of
these facilities, the annexation and proposed development of the subject site is
premature. The applicant/developer is required to pay for these improvements,
which are detailed and illustrated in the application materials. An annexation
agreement will be required to define the improvements, the costs, and the timing of
payment for these City-installed public improvements.
The proposed plat dedicates land for road and utility right of way for the Davidson
Avenue NE extension from its current terminus to a new intersection with 85th
Street NE. Davidson Avenue NE will be improved to an urban section per City
standards from 85th Street NE along the length of the DCPUD project limits.
Additional roadway dedication is proposed for Edmonson Avenue on the east
boundary of the site from 85th Street NE to the south extent of the project limits.
Edmonson will be improved to rural road section standards per the requirements of
Wright County, which has jurisdiction over this roadway.
Right of way and urban section construction is required for the project’s frontage on
85th Street NE from Edmonson Avenue to Trunk Highway 25.
An Intersection Control Evaluation is required by the Minnesota Department of
Transportation (MnDOT) for the 85th Street NE and Trunk Highway 25 intersection.
The applicant is required to provide and/or pay for the Intersection Control

Evaluation and the necessary right of way and construction of a roundabout as
dictated by the outcome of the evaluation.
The City’s subdivision ordinance requires information on the extension of utilities to
the site and for the subdivision. Utilities required to serve the project are provided in
new or expanded rights of way to the plat as noted above.
The DCPUD ordinance and subdivision ordinance also require that roads and utilities
are extended to the extent of the DCPUD property to provide opportunity for further
extension to serve future growth. The project plans provide for these extensions and
rights of way along 85th Street NE to TH 25, along the proposed Davidson Avenue NE
extension, along Edmonson Avenue, and a right of way and paved trail/utility access
across the south boundary of the proposed DCPUD. The City Engineer’s oﬃce has
commented on the necessary revision and detail of these utility and roadway plans.
Plat and lot easements are required per the subdivision ordinance, along with any
easement for public improvements as required by the City Engineer’s oﬃce.

Rezoning
•

•

•
•

•

Rezoning approvals necessary for the project include development of a speciﬁc
DCPUD zoning district adopted for the site, rezoning of the site to that district, as
well as rezoning of the smaller lots and outlots proposed in the plat.
Lot 1, Block 1 and Lot 2, Block 1 are proposed to be zoned DCPUD, with the speciﬁc
ordinance of the Edmonson Business Center Planned Unit Development (EBCDCPUD) applicable for the site.
Lot 1, Block 2 is proposed to be zoned IBC (Industrial Business Campus). Outlots A
and B are also proposed to be rezoned to IBC (Industrial and Business Campus).
Outlots C and D are proposed to be rezoned to Agriculture-Open Space (A-O) and will
also include the Shoreland Overlay District and Wetland Overlay District zoning upon
annexation. The A-O zoning designation supports the open space area and greenway
corridor for the community, and the requirement that the outlots are subject to
Shoreland regulations. Any future development proposal in this area would be
subject to more extensive review and detailed land use and zoning designations,
with land use applications as may be required.
The subject property is guided as Light Industrial Park (LIP), a land use category that
anticipates primarily I-1 (Light Industrial) or IBC (Industrial and Business Campus)
zoning. The Light Industrial Park designation also accommodates A-O (AgricultureOpen Space) zoning.

(3)(h)

Land Supply. The DCPUD will not
displace other land uses the City
deems important for the stable,
long-term growth of the
community, including other
industrial lands or other lands
critical to the achievement of the
City's long-range development
goals, and the City determines that
absorption of land area for data
center development is appropriate
based on the City's industrial and
other long-range land use
development goals as described in

This ordinance provision was designed to evaluate the
City’s supply of industrial land against large-scale data
center land use. Both short-term and long-term supplies
of industrial land are of critical interest to the City in
support of its 2040 Plan tax base and employment goals.
Industrial land absorption can be slower as compared to
other land uses. Development of industrial land with
utilities and streets and siting relative to other land uses
can be a challenge due to buﬀering needs and costs.
Industrial land use is also highly sensitive to location based
on the type of use. Topography that supports large sites or
buildings can also limit options. For these reasons,
Monticello has carefully planned and guided locations for
industrial land development
26

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

the Monticello 2040 Plan and other
adopted City planning documents.

The proposed Edmonson Business Center consumes
approximately 370 acres of developable industrial area as
data center development. The plans also identify 11 acres
of other light industrial use area outside the DCPUD area,
and more than 131 acres of land reserved outside of the
DCPUD as buﬀer within the Shoreland Overlay area. These
acreages, plus additional acreage for rights of way, total
approximately 547 acres of Light Industrial Park land
within the city’s growth area (MOAA).
Industrial land designations within the current City
boundaries are largely fully developed, with approximately
50 acres and a handful of parcels still available.
There are four primary areas of industrial land use on the
City’s 2040 Future Land Use Map in the growth area of the
MOAA, totaling approximately 980 acres. The proposed
Edmonson Business Center is one of those four. The others
include an area of approximately 38 acres of Light
Industrial Park adjoining Otter Creek Business Park, an
area of approximately 370 acres of Light Industrial Park
north of the Bertram Lakes area, and 570 acres guided
Employment Campus in the northwest of the MOAA.
Apart from the 38-acre site, all of these areas face
challenges in utility service, with connections to both
sewer and water often miles away. This includes the
current DCPUD proposal area. This development
application proposes paying for and extending the
necessary utilities, including a long-planned extension of
sanitary sewer along Fallon Avenue (a distance of nearly
two miles). Without these improvements, the subject site
would also need to await City-provided utility extensions.
The utilities and transportation improvements further
serve to beneﬁt a signiﬁcant portion of the City’s southerly
growth area.
Thus, while the proposed project consumes approximately
one-third of the undeveloped land currently designated as
industrial and half of that guided as Light Industrial Park, it
does so without consuming immediately developable
industrial land.
In regard to adding industrial development capacity, the
City prepared a Feasibility Study in 2022 that prioritizes
industrial land development to the northwest area of the
27

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

MOAA. The City has also begun capital investment to serve
that approximately 1000 acres of land, completing a
watermain extension along CSAH 39 West earlier this year.
Criterion met, pending adoption of ﬁndings that the
proposed land consumption for a single light industrial
use is appropriate and that the proposed development is
not consuming land that would have otherwise been
immediately available for industrial development. These
ﬁndings have been incorporated into the resolution for
approval.
(3)(i)

Public Beneﬁt. The DCPUD will
provide identiﬁable public beneﬁts,
including the creation and
maintenance of tax base, and will
avoid negative impacts such as
those identiﬁed in this section, over
the long term.

The applicant suggests that there are several categories of
public beneﬁt that result from the project.
First is the additional tax base generated by the data
center. The applicant has provided an analysis of
estimated tax base impact. The City’s ﬁnance department
has also analyzed the expected tax base created by this
project in a companion analysis to this report (See Exhibit
J). The analysis under conservative estimates indicates
signiﬁcant positive impact on the current tax base. The
diversiﬁcation and continued strategic transition from a
tax base dependent on a single-large taxpayer (Xcel) is a
stated goal of the City.
Based on the past rate of industrial land development for
the community, it is likely that the development of this site
in the near-term will generate tax impact more quickly as a
data center campus than as compared to development of
other individual light industrial development uses. Further,
that tax impact would be minimized by the signiﬁcant
infrastructure investment that would be needed to extend
services to the development area.
The applicant has also identiﬁed approximately $70 million
dollars in oﬀ-site and adjacent public infrastructure that
would be constructed to beneﬁt this project. This
infrastructure is based on the City’s adopted long-range
utility plans and will also be available for extension to
other lands (See Exhibit S). The utility extensions also
support additional upgrade to existing roadway networks.
These investments defray potential City borrowing costs
and oversizing costs typically paid for by the City.
Separately, Xcel Energy has indicated that the regional
transmission line construction necessary to support this
28

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

project creates added reliability and system updates for
existing ratepayers without passing that cost on to
customers.
Based on the traﬃc information provided, this project will
have a signiﬁcantly lower traﬃc generation for local roads
than would a comparative industrial park project.
Added to this are the extraordinary setbacks and
screening built into the DCPUD zoning district, not typical
of other industrial districts.
The Monticello 2040 Plan also recognizes the need to
provide and preserve open space lands. Preservation of
the south Shoreland Overlay area not only provides a
buﬀer to the proposed use but also preserves open space
along a natural amenity, including a publicly accessible
greenway corridor. Further, the development requires
completion and extension of an extensive set of trail
improvements, including a trail along the southerly
greenway corridor.
Finally, with regard to the last clause in this requirement,
the City retains signiﬁcant enforcement ability in the event
the project development creates conﬂicts. Both the PUD
zoning approach and the CUP approvals grant the City
broad authority to ensure that ongoing compliance with
ordinance standards will be maintained.
Criterion met, pending a ﬁnding in support of the
beneﬁts as described, which have been incorporated into
the resolutions for approval.

(3)(m)

2040 Plan. The DCPUD will not
conﬂict with other elements of the
City's Comprehensive Plan.

The proposed data center site continues a pattern of
commercial and industrial use in the Highway 25 corridor.
The impacts and beneﬁts of land development as a data
center campus in the Light Industrial District are described
in section (3)(h) and (3)(i).
The 2040 Comprehensive Plan seeks to “Ensure the
managed development of appropriate and compatible
land uses which is resilient to shifts and changes in the
economy, real estate market and consumer demand, and
responds to a changing tax base.”
The City has developed an extensive list of performance
requirements to address compatibility where diﬀering land
uses abut one another. These standards increase where
residential uses abut industrial uses. The DCPUD
intensiﬁes this eﬀort. Setbacks are typically 5 to 6 (or
more) times the normal industrial setback. Buﬀering is
substantially greater, both through distance and through
the components of the buﬀer (berm, landscape, etc.).
Landscape screening is required to be planted in all buﬀers
– in the DCPUD it carries the additional requirement that it
30

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

is planted at a size to reach full visual screening within
three years. The Comprehensive Plan and Zoning
regulations are clear that projects unable to comply with
these enhanced standards are not eligible for approval. In
this regard, the protections of the zoning regulations in
the DCPUD further the intent of the Comprehensive Plan
for land use compatibility.
The Plan’s statements on resiliency to market changes and
demand and its statement regarding a changing tax base
are relevant to this application, as the proposed use would
provide both tax base stabilization and diversiﬁcation of
industrial use.
The DCPUD’s treatment of the lands along the southerly
water basin also provides the opportunity for public access
to this open space resource, in alignment with the 2040
Plan’s goals for an open space “frame” for the community.
Criterion met, pending ﬁndings noted for 2040 Plan
consistency.

The applicant has presented a plan that shows a total area
of Lot 1, Block 1 (the parcel containing the DCPUD data
center site) at 364 acres according to the Preliminary Plat.
By deﬁnition in the DCPUD, a minimum Floor Area Ratio
(FAR) is required to be established based on the net
developable area of the subject parcel. DCPUD FAR is
speciﬁcally deﬁned by the DCPUD ordinance and excludes
undevelopable land in the DCPUD, including ponds, lakes,
wetlands, right of way, electrical substation, and required
building setbacks.
The applicant has identiﬁed a net FAR area of 208.62
acres, thus requiring a minimum FAR of .23 (misstated in
the applicant’s narrative as .22). Although the area is not
speciﬁcally dimensioned on the applicant’s “Site
Improvements Plan” exhibit (Exhibit I4), the shaded white
area on that drawing appears to represent the 208.62
acres of “Developable Area” for DCPUD FAR. This aspect
of the submission should be veriﬁed to conﬁrm the
applicable FAR for this project and the resulting required
square footage. This clariﬁcation should be made following
the ﬁnal location and size of the substation parcel.
Up to 3,000,000 square feet of building development
represents the maximum as studied by the Alternative
Urban Areawide Review (AUAR), the relevant
environmental study for the project area.

Back-up Power. Back-up power
generators within the DCPUD may
only be used for back-up electrical
supply during a power outage.

voluntary shutdown of power is not

requirements of this section. Such
storage shall comply with all MPCA

and all applicable codes and
standards such as NFPA 58.

The applicant has stated in their application materials that
the DCPUD will not utilize diesel back-up power
generation greater than 25 MW. The DCPUD ordinance for
this site sets this as a requirement for the site which may
not be exceeded. Backup diesel generation below 25MW
does not require separate PUC review for power
generation. Staﬀ would further recommend that the
DCPUD utilize the generators meeting the lowest emission
threshold.

(10)(j)

Screening. Any outdoor facilities or
equipment such as back-up
generators, parking and private
circulation areas, temporary
construction staging areas and
temporary buildings or structures,
other mechanical equipment
regardless of location, security or

within 200' of the DCPUD property

100% opacity when viewed at
comparable grade from abutting
property used, zoned, or guided for

from the public right of way, with

subject to the following additional

i. Landscaping materials used for

the minimum site landscaping

ii. Landscaping utilized for

within 3 years of planting.

The applicant has provided a landscaping plan for the site,
including the berm area and areas outside of the berm. At
the time of Site Plan submission, a landscaping plan for
the interior of the site will be required.
The property is subject to the City’s landscaping
ordinance requirements, as well as the Shade Tree
Ordinance, Tree Manual, and canopy management
documents. In summary, staﬀ recommend additional or
modiﬁed tree planting that is consistent with the
following comments and recommendations:
1. DBH Removals & Mitigation inventory conﬁrms 45
specimen trees removed, totaling 810 caliper
inches mitigation required. Preservation credits
equal 1,127 caliper inches × 1.25 = 1,409 caliper
inches. After credits, 599 caliper inches of new
tree plantings are required.
2. Planting Plan provides 289 deciduous canopy trees
at 2" caliper (=578 cal/in) and 1,067 ornamentals
at 1.5" caliper (=1,600 cal/in). Together, this equals
=2,178 cal/in of new plantings, exceeding the 599
cal/in requirement. Conifers (1,437 at 6 ft. B&B)
and 8,800 shrubs meet buﬀer and opacity
requirements.
3. Buﬀer Requirements for canopy, understory, and
shrub counts meet ordinance thresholds.
Year-round opacity is documented as 100%
provided. However, as straight-line storms in
Monticello typically come from the west and
northwest, positioning is critical for a thriving
42

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

iii. Screening may be accomplished
through individual or combination
use of fencing or walls which meet
the materials requirements of this
section, landscaping, and/or
berming.
iv. Landscaping materials and
berming used for screening may be
located within the required
structure setback; fences and walls
are subject to the structure setback
above.
v. In all cases, fences or walls used
for screening purposes must install
the required perimeter buﬀer
landscaping materials of this
section on the exterior side of the
wall.
vi. Chain link fence with slats shall
not be used for screening purposes.

planting. Conifers planted on the top of berms are
most exposed to wind shear and shallow soils,
creating higher failure risk. The goal is screening
and resilience, conifers should be placed on the
berm sides, particularly east and southeast slopes,
where soil depth and shelter improve survival.
Staggered, mixed-species rows (cedar, pine,
spruce) are preferred over straight lines to diﬀuse
wind force and strengthen the screen. Shade trees
should be layered behind conifers in staggered
rows, oﬀset to interlock canopies.
4. Species aligns with the Tree Manual. Approved
canopy and ornamental species are included,
shrubs and groundcovers are native and
compliant, and no prohibited species are listed.
However, maples and crabapple ornamentals are
heavily represented. Inventory data already shows
Monticello exceeding the 10-20-30 diversity
guideline for maples and ash. The staﬀ
recommendation is to increase canopy diversity
with oaks, hackberry, hybrid elms, and other
natives listed in the City’s tree manual, while
trimming back ornamental counts.
5. Canopy vs. Understory balance requirements are
exceeded. However, canopy trees are
underrepresented (7–8% of total) compared to
ornamentals (55%). Ordinance intent and canopy
expansion goals emphasize canopy-builders.
Adjustments should increase canopy tree
proportion to 30–35%, reduce ornamentals to 20–
25%, and maintain conifers at 30–35%. Staﬀ notes
that where power line corridors may interfere with
planting plans, a customized screening plan must
be developed at the time of Site Plan review.
6. Formal DBH replacement schedule within the
landscape plan summarizing caliper inches of new
trees should exclude shrubs and clarify
height-based listings (such as conifers at 6 ft.
B&B). This ensures how canopy and ornamental
trees contribute to mitigation. The DCPUD
requires that plant materials required for

43

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

screening must be sized to provide a full screen
within three years of planting.
7. Enhance native buﬀer plantings beyond minimum
compliance. The applicant’s plans should be
amended to add hardwood seeding, prairie mixes,
wildﬂower prairies, and shrub thickets. These
additions will strengthen natural views, improve
biodiversity, and provide long-term resilience.
8. Install a dedicated irrigation line for berm
plantings to ensure proper establishment and
long-term survival. This irrigation may be
temporary until plant materials are established
and inspected as healthy. Landscape irrigation
water volumes are not subject to the 75,000 GPD
limits applied to the operation of the data center
project itself.

Rezoning to IBC and A-O Analysis
The predominant zoning on the property will be a new zoning district identiﬁed as “Edmonson
Business Center – DCPUD” that incorporates the various elements of the data center project
area. Two other zoning districts are also proposed on the site.
In the northwest corner of the property, approximately 11.2 acres would be rezoned to IBC,
Industrial and Business Campus (IBC). The IBC district is intended to support light industrial
uses, as well as certain supporting commercial uses, such as hospitality development. It is a
district that speciﬁes higher site and building amenities than other industrial districts and is
generally compatible with both industrial and commercial locations.
The zoning area includes a lot to be platted as Lot 1, Block 2 and Outlots A and B. The IBC
District is an appropriate zoning district per the Light Industrial Park land use designation for
this area. Access to future development on these parcels would be via the proposed Davidson
Avenue NE extension.
The two large outlots (Outlots C and D) along the south boundary of the plat are proposed to be
zoned A-O, Agriculture-Open Space. These areas are encumbered by the Shoreland Overlay
applicable upon annexation. The lake is to be classiﬁed with a Natural Environment designation
by the Minnesota DNR. Under Shoreland regulations, industrial uses are not allowed in this
area, despite the underlying LIP land use designation. As such, the A-O zoning is appropriate at
this time. Any future development of the outlots would be subject to the Shoreland Overlay
ordinance and platting.
Plat Analysis
The City’s subdivision ordinance governs the requirements and review of the preliminary plat.
The City also uses its Design Manual and Construction Standards to evaluate the proposed
design of the public infrastructure plans submitted as part of the preliminary plat requirements.
As discussed above, the proposed preliminary plat consists of 3 developable lots and 4 outlots.
Lot 1 Block 1 is the largest of the development parcels totaling 363.6 acres. This parcel proposed
to be rezoned to the DCPUD district. Lot 2, Block 1 is shown on the plat to be 6.6 acres, also
zoned DCPUD. The intended use of the parcel is stated as an electrical substation. The applicant
has indicated that the speciﬁc size and location of this parcel may change prior to ﬁnal plat. Lot
1, Block 2 is a 2.6-acre parcel in the northwest corner of the plat, to be conveyed to the City for
future public utility purposes.
The proposed Outlots A and B are 4.4 acres and 4.2 acres in size, respectively. These parcels are
to be rezoned IBC and provide a combination of future developable area (shown in red on the
preliminary plat) and undevelopable area, and additional buﬀer area for the foreseeable future.

48

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

These areas are shown in grey on the preliminary plat. Any future development of these outlots
would require replatting and consideration of appropriate development per the subject zoning.
Outlots C and D are areas in the south and southeast portions of the plat, generally reﬂecting
the areas subject to Shoreland Overlay zoning, which is measured at 1000’ from the ordinary
high water level of the water basin. Outlot C is 101.7 acres is size and reﬂects the larger upland
portion of the shoreland area. Outlot D is 29.8 acres in size and encompasses the public water
basin and smaller areas of upland around the shoreline.
The plat also provides right of way for public street purposes along Davidson Avenue, Edmonson
Avenue, and 85th Street NE, as well as a 150-foot wide right of way along the north shore of the
public water basin between Davidson and Edmonson that would create a utility corridor and
trail. Staﬀ notes that the label identiﬁes this area as a “Future Right of Way” – the “future”
portion should be removed. Staﬀ recommend that this area is relabeled as a separate outlot to
be conveyed to the City with a drainage/utility easement and trail easement as a part of the
description, rather than right of way.
RECOMMENDED ACTION
Staff recommend approval of the proposed rezoning to DCPUD, IBC and A-O for the specified
parcels and DCPUD Conditional Use Permit. Staff also recommend approval of the preliminary
plat. The recommendations for approval are based on proposed findings of fact provided in the
accompanying resolutions.
The findings in the resolutions are based on the 2040 Vision + Plan’s policies and goals as the
guiding land use policy of the City. The 2040 Vision + Plan sets the direction for the best
interests of the City as a whole, and is a collection of integrated plans, goals, policies, and
standards, none of which stand on their own.
An ordinance has been drafted for the Edmonson Business Center Data Center Planned Unit
Development. The ordinance provides the zoning standards applicable under the general
DCPUD zoning ordinance, as well as those necessary to regulate this specific site.
A list of operational and physical changes to the plans and proposals, and the requirements for
supplemental information are provided in Exhibit Z of this report and incorporated in the
decision resolutions. These conditions are subject to review and amendment by the Planning
Commission and City Council. Some conditions will require resolution prior to Final Plat
submission, or are required at phased Site Plan review, while others will carry forward as
maintained conditions for the life of the PUD/CUP. The timing for resolution of outstanding
issues is noted as part of the condition.
Any application approval incorporates the adopted ordinance, all conditions, the minimum
standards of the DCPUD ordinance, and other applicable City Code provisions as approved by
49

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

the City Council. No approvals are final until all conditions are met and/or financially secured
under the applicable agreements.
Although each of the application decisions reviewed in this report requires a speciﬁc set of
ﬁndings and separate recommendation, they constitute a uniﬁed package. It is unlikely that any
individual application would proceed without each of the others. The staﬀ recommendation
incorporates a cross-approval condition that requires approval of all requests to proceed with
the project.
An annexation agreement specifying the terms and conditions of the annexation of the
property will be considered by City Council with the annexation petitions and will accompany
the application decisions for rezoning, conditional use permit, and preliminary plat.
A development contract specifying the terms and conditions of the subdivision approval will be
considered by the City Council with the final plat.

materials, or other elements. Removal of such improvements shall require
immediate restoration of the site subject to approval of any phase of development
in that location and in accordance with the approved phasing plan.
n. No construction activity shall be allowed in any portion of the project, temporary or
otherwise, for which a Site Plan with phasing has not been submitted and approved.
18.

No wetland impacts are permitted within the plat and EBC-DCPUD area, excepting
submission of a wetland sequencing application prior to final plat, or such modification
required as part of a PUC permitting process.

19.

The applicant must supply a wetland vegetation management plan for wetlands within
the DCPUD area.

20.

The applicant shall prepare at their sole cost the Intersection Control Evaluation
required by MnDOT in their comment letter of September 29, 2026 and shall provide for
the implementation of the infrastructure recommendations therein at their sole cost.

21.

The applicant shall prepare a revised Ambient Noise Study which complies with MPCA
Guidelines for a one-week period prior to construction of the berm.

22.

Subsequent sound studies shall adhere to Ordinance 865-DCPUD and with the following
additional requirements:
a. An independent, third-party engineer shall prepare the required sound studies and
resulting mitigation plans demonstrating compliance with MPCA Rules, which shall
be subject to the review and approval of the City. The developer shall provide a
security for such review.

56

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

b. Operational noise study shall be provided with the first phase building construction.
c. Once operational, sound studies per Ordinance 865-DCPUD shall be completed in
July of each year and with each phase of principal building construction.
d. A Noise Impact Study per City Ordinance §130.09 shall be prepared for lowfrequency sound by an independent, third-party engineer. Such study shall be
accompanied by a subsequent mitigation plan, which shall be subject to the review
of the City’s designated sound engineer consultant and approval of the City. The
developer shall provide a security for such review.
23.

The applicant shall designate a liaison for reports of noise violations and complaints
emanating from the DCPUD with the first Site Plan Approval and update such contact
information throughout the life of the project with the City’s Department of Building
Safety & Code Enforcement. Such liaison shall be responsible for evaluating response
and communicating information on any required mitigation.

24.

Right of Way dedication for the plat shall be required as follows:
a. Right of way for Davidson Avenue NE to be dedicated to full width per City
Engineer’s report, and constructed to urban standard to the limits of the plat.
b. Right of way for 85th Street NE to be dedicated for the full width (as needed) per the
City Engineer’s report, and constructed to urban standard to the limits of the plat.
c. Right of way for Edmonson Avenue NE to be dedicated for the full width and
constructed to a rural standard per the requirements of Wright County and the City
Engineer’s report.
d. If an outlot is not provided, right of way for utilities shall be provided between
Outlots C and D at a width of 150’.
e. Right of way as required by the City Engineer to serve required or future
roundabouts at the intersection of 85th Street NE and the intersection of TH 25 and
85th Street NE and Edmonson Avenue.

25.

The plat shall be updated to reflect the required 6’ lot and 12’ plat perimeter easements
per the Subdivision Ordinance.

26.

Berm Requirements:
a. The applicant shall provide for construction of the berm and related screening
plantings as a first phase of work on the site, prior to any other development work.
Screening requirements of the DCPUD shall be waived for grading and construction
of the berm. The City may approve other development work, at its sole discretion,

57

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

once screening has been established and would be effective in portions of the EBCDCPUD.
b. Construction of the berm shall start in areas closest to adjoining residential uses.
c. Height of the berm must be adjusted to demonstrate compliance with the City’s
requirement that berm is no less than 10 feet above adjoining property lines, and/or
the adjoining roadway centerline, as applicable.
d. The applicant shall provide for temporary irrigation for landscape plantings on the
berm to enhance survival of the materials. Such irrigation shall be exempt from the
75,000 gallons per day water use otherwise applicable for the project.
e. The berm shall be placed to the inside of the required drainage and utility
easements on the site.
27.

Landscaping and screening for the DCPUD shall be revised to be consistent with the
following requirements:
a. Tree removals shall be limited to those shown on the Tree Removal Plan.
b. Revise landscaping plan to place conifers on the berm sides, particularly east and
southeast slopes, where soil depth and shelter improve survival. Plantings should be
shown in staggered, mixed-species rows (cedar, pine, spruce) diffuse wind force and
strengthen the screen. Shade trees are to be layered behind conifers in staggered
rows, offset to interlock canopies.
c. Increase canopy diversity with oaks, hackberry, hybrid elms, and other natives listed
in the City’s tree manual, while trimming back ornamental counts.
d. Increase canopy tree proportion to 30–35%, reduce ornamentals to 20–25%, and
maintain conifers at 30–35%.
e. Where power line corridors may interfere with planting plans, a customized
screening plan must be developed at the time of Site Plan review.
f. Exclude shrubs from DBH replacement schedule and clarify height-based listings
(such as conifers at 6 ft. B&B). Recalculate and revise landscaping plan to meet
replacement.
g. Revise plan to provide planting specification requiring lant materials to be sized to
provide a full screen within three years of planting.
h. Enhance native buffer plantings beyond minimum compliance. The applicant’s plans
should be amended to add hardwood seeding, prairie mixes, wildflower prairies, and

58

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

shrub thickets. These additions will strengthen natural views, improve biodiversity,
and provide long term resilience.
i.

Install a dedicated irrigation line for berm plantings to ensure proper establishment
and long term survival. This irrigation may be temporary until plant materials are
established and healthy.

j.

Show seeding specifications in all locations.

k. Additional landscaping within the Developable Area will be reviewed at the time of
Site Plan review.
28.

A warranty deed shall be provided conveying the proposed Lot 1, Block 2 to the City of
Monticello.

29.

In lieu of right of way, a warranty deed shall be provided conveying 150’ in width for the
public utility and pathway corridor between Outlots C and D.

30.

Accessory Battery Energy Storage Systems, if employed, shall require a separate
emergency services evaluation to be provided with the Site Plan review application
applicable to its installation and payment for any related preparedness shall be required
as determined by the City.

31.

Trash enclosures shall be located within buildings meeting the materials requirements
of the City’s zoning ordinance and the DCPUD and shall be located within the
Developable Area of the Site Improvements Plan.

32.

Site landscaping is completed as approved, with required maintenance securities as
specified in the Zoning Ordinance. Compliance with the approved landscaping plan is an
ongoing requirement of CUP and site plan approval. Materials that are removed or fail
to survive shall be replaced, regardless of landscape guarantees.

33.

The applicant shall execute and fulfill the terms of an annexation agreement and
development contract will provides for the applicant’s responsibility for all costs
associated with the development and operation of the project, including municipal
utilities, transportation, and any other public services or infrastructure necessary to
support the DCPUD. This includes infrastructure improvements or mitigation for the
expected public infrastructure impact or capacity increase created or required by the
DCPUD, and will identify a timeline satisfactory to the City for the submission of
payments and securities for such infrastructure.

34.

All public improvement plans are to be prepared at the applicant’s expense, whether
publicly or privately installed, and regardless of location.

59

Planning Commission Agenda – 10/06/2026

35.

Provide opportunities for, and coordinate with, the City’s decision to provide Fibernet
installations in adjoining rights of way (installation at City cost and discretion).

36.

Existing irrigation wells on site are required to be capped in accordance with the
appropriate agency requirements

37.

The applicant and Xcel Energy shall execute a shared access agreement providing for
such access and maintenance of the shared access improvements to the electrical
substation.

38.

Any approvals granted under this application shall incorporate each of the required
mitigation measures of the AUAR Mitigation Plan adopted conducted for this project
area.

39.

addressed by this application or the DCPUD ordinance, shall be met.

consideration of the Final Plat.

with the terms and conditions of the City Council approvals.

Compliance with the terms of the City’s Engineering Staff letter dated September 28,
2026, and including the Mitigation Plan Comments of September 14, 2028.

Comments and recommendations of other Staff and Planning Commission.
